tbmm-2016-02-27sit02spe0145par0009	Mr. President, I have received a pledge from our group on behalf of the National Intelligence Agency Undersecretary budget from institutions involved in the first round of the 2016 Central Management Budget Bill. In this honor, I salute your high council. As it is known in English and French, the word "intelligent" means "intelligent," meaning "intellectual," "insurgency" in Turkish for "intellectual." In today's modern - day world, intelligence has been described as a product of the processing of information and documents from various sources in response to the needs set by the state. Intelligence is one of the key and key factors that affect the present and future of governments, as well as in the past, because governments conduct this activity to uncover the capabilities of the target state, the organisation, the group, or individuals, to predict their possible course of action. Governments capable of producing healthy intelligence can see the future more clearly, have prior knowledge of possible problems, and have access to clear facts. All of these results can't be achieved just by gathering news, information, and documents. They all need to be processed in a certain systematic form. Unsubstantiated news, information or document can lead a state to misleading consequences and decisions. Thus, raw information is to be evaluated and processed by the process of computing, approving, commenting, and publishing. All these activities are a continuous work and are made up of four basic stages. One: the detection of intelligence needs and the manipulation of collection efforts. Two: gathering news. Three: the processing of the news. Four: publication and use of intelligence. However, if these four stages can be managed in a healthy manner, a successful intelligence can be obtained. In modern times, especially major states have given regular operations of the intelligence wheel to the intelligence organisations they've established, providing all kinds of financial and political support so that these organisations can work more efficiently. On 6 January 1926, the Great Republic of Turkey established the head of the National Security Service, as well as the establishment of a modern intelligence organisation in developed states. This institution has had several minor structural changes, depending on the needs needed, and has carried out our country's intelligence activity by 1965. On 22 July 1965, the National Intelligence Agency was established to ensure that information on the preparation of the state's national security policy and 644, adopted by the Grand National Assembly of Turkey, could be collected with one hand. The agency's task is to gather information about the indivisible integrity of the Republic of Turkey, its constitutional order, its existence, its independence, its security, and all its elements that create its national power, from within and outside, and take precautions, and to warn the authorities of the necessary situations. The law stipulates that the institution will be run by an undersecretary and that the undersecretary will be responsible to the Prime Minister for carrying out the duties set by law. The institution, which operates in accordance with Law provisions 644 for 19 years, needed a new arrangement in light of the changing and developing conditions, which was adopted on November 1, 1983 by State Intelligence Services 2937 and the National Intelligence Service Act, and was adopted on January 1, 1984. The budget of the National Intelligence Agency's Undersecretary for 2016 is set for $1 billion 636m,803,000 Turkish liras. There is a 47 per cent increase compared to 2015. This increase stems from the rise of costs and the new service building, which is being built for the organization. In our country and in our recent geography, recent events and difficulties have once again demonstrated the importance of a powerful intelligence organization. Our party has full faith and confidence that the National Intelligence Agency will continue to successfully combat any threat to our country, domestic and external. Of course, the power of the National Intelligence Agency will increase with the support it will see from our community. I wish the National Intelligence Agency's budget of 2016 to be better, and I salute your grand delegation.

The countries, such as Britain, Germany, Italy, Japan, India, Australia, are governed by parliamentary democracy. Besides, there is no evidence, no scientific data, that there is a direct link between the level of democracy in any country and the presidential system. By the way, in this regard, we see that some of the facts that have been made with scientific results are actually done by a think tank called Global Policy and Strategy. The results of this organization are very important. Following their research, the results of the country's management models have been striking. Not all of them, according to these results, but I'll give you a summary. Many countries, including the United States, implementing the presidential system, have found that many indicators of development, such as democracy and economics, remain behind in the world's rankings. For example, in all democratic and economic development indicators, the majority of Central and South African countries, which are at the end of the world's rankings, are run by the presidential system. In contrast, the majority of European and Asian Pacific countries in the world's rankings, such as Norway, the Netherlands and Australia, are governed by parliamentary systems. Thus, the countries implementing the presidential system are generally behind in the world's rankings. The example of the United States is an exception. It has now been established that countries with parliamentary systems worldwide are more successful in all indicators of development. The majority of countries around the world, such as NATO, G8, G20 and the EU, are governed by the parliamentary system, once again, when the governing systems of countries that are part of international and national organisations based on economic-political business units are evaluated. Looking at the indexes on the world scale, it is clear from this research that democracy and the understanding of law in countries run by the parliamentary system are far more advanced than the countries run by the presidential system. In addition, countries with parliamentary systems are more successful worldwide in the analysis of the rule of law, democratic development, individual freedom, and freedom of the press. A similar picture arises when countries are assessed in terms of economic development. Analyses of countries' prosperity levels and economic development indexes have made it very clear that countries with parliamentary systems are much better off than countries with economic levels of development. The majority of countries run by the world's presidential system, particularly the Central and South African countries, which are in serious economic difficulties, are far behind in the world's ranking for economic development. One of the factors that determines the quality of life for people living in countries is social life, as you know. According to research studies of human development, social development, and social capital indexes, people living in parliamentary systems have far better possibilities than those in countries run by the presidential system. The results of the analysis of education levels, health care services, and ▪ Security indexes show the obvious superiority of countries governed by the parliamentary system again. This conclusion shows that the quality of health, education and security services provided to citizens in countries with parliamentary systems is much higher than those in countries with the presidential system. So the fact that countries are governed by good governance principles and have political stability is among the other key factors that show the country's success. The data on the country's vulnerability index, which has been analysed under the scope of research, has also made it very clear that the parliamentary system states are in a much better position than the presidential system. Most of the country's governances with the parliamentary system have been seen to have political and political stability and have been governed by the good governance principles of the countries. In contrast, it turns out that the majority of countries run by the presidential system are in political and political instability and that the country's governments are facing major problems. The other thing is, it's for the Human Rights Foundation. First of all, our Human Rights Agency is administratively and financially autonomous. As in many other examples of countries -- I have just expressed in my speech -- the board structure, appointed by the Council of Ministers and the presidency. There are many examples of this in Europe. However, the use of a statement like this is not an accurate expression for our institution, but an administrative and financial autonomous institution. As for the human rights and equality institution, the new institution that we're going to build, you know that a bill for fighting discrimination has been drafted, and then it has been decided that the Human Rights Institution will be united and that there will be one institution. The reason for this was that you can't distinguish human rights from discrimination, and you can't distinguish discrimination from human rights. So when we look at other countries that might be examples, we see that countries, especially in many European countries, are run by the same institution as human rights and discrimination. In this framework, we decided to combine the institution we would create to fight discrimination with the Human Rights Institution and turn it into a single institution. We call it the human rights and equality institution. This institution will perform three basic functions. One, the matter of human rights will be authorized to conduct resen reviews. Two, it will be able to work as a national prevention mechanism for torture and abuse, and it will be relevant to the United Nations Additional Elders Protocol. Third, they will be able to fight discrimination. As for the structure of the institution, I've explained that in the Human Rights Commission, we're creating an extremely flexible structure. We've given the board the authority of the board, the office heads, the relevant departments, and what tasks those units can take over. Traditionally, when you look at our institutions, that authority is regulated by law. For example, a department head, the office head's duties, all of which are organised by law, and there is a process under that law. But what are the responsibilities in the law, what are the tasks, and we've determined them, and we've given the board a very broad mandate in this framework, and we've created a flexible structure in the idea that the institution can be flexible within itself in terms of future demands. What I want to say here is that it's not how and how the appointment was made, it's the functional independence of that institution that matters. So, once the institution starts working, it's functional independence. Yes, we said that the Human Rights Foundation would be activated, but you can appreciate that there is no way that that Human Rights Agency can function effectively with part-time board members and about 15-20 personnel. So, what did we do? We've raised the staff of the Human Rights and Equality Association to 150.
